### Method (honest)

Read from the extracted text layer, page by page, using a per-page digest checked against the full text of every unit, plus full-text reads of the worked examples and answer pages where errors are reported. Several pages use a font whose text layer extracts as non-Latin glyphs (notably the simultaneous-equations pages and some fraction answers); those pages were checked from rendered images and are legible in print — the garbling is a text-layer defect, not a print defect. Exercise question lists were counted and skimmed, not read word-by-word; all exposition, teaching notes and answer keys were read in full. Keyword searches over the whole text layer back the "does not teach" list.

### Companion verdict: YES — it companions the Secondary 1 Student's Book

Same five units in the same order, keyed to the same student-book pages (the guide repeatedly cites student-book page numbers, e.g. "the definitions on page 40 and 41"). It is a genuine companion, with two numbering/coverage breaks (see below):

- **Guide's exercise numbering does NOT match the student's in Unit 4.** The guide adds a single-variable-equations exercise it calls "Exercise 6", and calls the simultaneous-equations set "Exercise 7". In the student book the simultaneous-equations set IS Exercise 6 (printed p. 105) and Exercise 7 is the inequalities set (printed p. 108). A teacher following the guide's numbers will be one exercise out from the student book in Unit 4.
- **The guide does NOT cover inequalities at all.** The student book teaches forming and solving inequalities (printed pp. 106–108, Exercise 7, eight questions). The guide's Unit 4 "Learn About" and "Learning Outcomes" both promise inequalities ("solve inequalities in a range of situations", "Formation and solution of inequalities"), but the guide jumps from simultaneous equations (printed pp. 85–87) straight to Unit 5 Statistics (printed p. 88). No teaching notes and no answers for inequalities exist in this guide.

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages are the GUIDE's own pagination)

**Front matter (printed pp. 1–4).** Introduction on teaching mathematics in changing conditions (p. 1); "About this guide" — purpose, how to use it (p. 2); "Making Classroom Assessment" — product/process/assessment notes (pp. 3–4).

**Unit 1 — Natural numbers, factors and proportion (printed pp. 5–31).** Syllabus front-matter: Learn About, key inquiry questions, learning outcomes, competencies, links to other subjects (pp. 5–6). Then teaching notes and "Expected answers" for each student exercise in turn: place value, odd/even, prime/composite, HCF/LCM (pp. 7–11); fractions — addition, subtraction, multiplication, division, reciprocals, fractions/percentages/decimals, calculator fraction keys (pp. 12–20); standard form, including calculating with standard form (pp. 20–22); percentages (pp. 22–24); profit and loss (pp. 25–27). **Guide-only extra:** discount and commission (pp. 28–30) — worked discount example (list price SSP 62.50, 12% discount → SSP 7.50 off, net SSP 55.00) and a commission example (agent sells property for SSP 50,000 at 7.5%) — neither topic is in the student book. Project work guidance (p. 31).

**Unit 2 — Measurement (printed pp. 32–50).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 32–33); teaching notes and expected answers for conversion of units, perimeter, circumference, area, circle area (pp. 34–40); surface area including cylinders and spheres, with a note on Archimedes (pp. 41–46); volume, capacity and the volume–capacity link (pp. 47–50).

**Unit 3 — Geometry and trigonometry (printed pp. 51–67).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 51–52); angles of triangles and quadrilaterals, isosceles-triangle task (pp. 53–55); transformations — translation, rotation, reflection (pp. 56–57); enlargement, scale factors, centre of enlargement, similar figures (pp. 58–60, going beyond the student book's brief treatment); bearings and scale drawings (pp. 61–63); trigonometry and angles of elevation/depression, with a fully worked tower-height example (pp. 63–67).

**Unit 4 — Algebra (printed pp. 68–87).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 68–69); simplifying expressions, substitution, negative numbers (pp. 70–73); expansion and factorising with expected answers (pp. 74–80); equation terminology in a web-article voice — "Math with letters is really just an extension of math without letters" (pp. 83–84, plainly lifted from an online source, a sharp tone break from the rest of the guide); solving single-variable equations (pp. 84–85); simultaneous equations with expected answers (pp. 85–87). Inequalities are promised but never delivered (see companion verdict).

**Unit 5 — Statistics (printed pp. 88–104).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 88–89); a general essay on what statistics is, measures of central tendency (pp. 90–93); probability — experiments, sample space, theoretical probability (pp. 94–101); expected answers for the probability exercises, including tree diagrams (pp. 101–104). The guide ends on printed p. 104 mid-answer-key (the last tree-diagram answers), with no closing section.

### What this book does NOT teach

Same absences as its student book (no complex numbers, matrices, calculus, surds, logarithms, Pythagoras' theorem, sets/Venn, functions, probability distributions), plus: it adds no new mathematical topic beyond discount/commission, enlargement/similar figures and the probability background essay. The inequalities gap above is a coverage gap, not a syllabus absence.

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- **Printed p. 87, "Expected answers" for the simultaneous-equations exercise:** the printed rows do not match the nine systems on printed p. 85 (which are the student book's Exercise 6, printed p. 105). Row "2" lists nine bare x-values and row "3" six bare x-values, with no y-values at all for two-variable systems. The correct solutions are: (a) x = 2, y = 3; (b) x = 1, y = 2; (c) x = −5, y = 22/3; (d) x = 2, y = −5; (e) x = 1, y = −3; (f) x = −3, y = 2; (g) x = 4, y = 1; (h) x = −3, y = 4; (i) x = 5, y = −3. The printed rows reproduce none of these pairs; they appear to belong to a different question set. Row "1" (x = 3, 9, 5, 14, 1, 2) answers the guide's own single-variable exercise, which has no student-book counterpart.
- **Printed p. 67, Exercise 15 answers, item 9:** "Do not do this problem" — a stray instruction copied from a source answer key, left in the list of answers. (The worked tower-height example on pp. 66–67 itself is correct: the tower is 90 m.)
- The discount worked example (printed p. 29: SSP 62.50 less 12% = SSP 55.00) was checked and is correct.

### Held-copy defects

- **Folio errors:** printed p. 16 appears twice in a row (PDF pp. 23–24); printed p. 86 is skipped (p. 85 followed by p. 87). Content flows continuously — no page is missing; only the numbering is wrong.
- **Text-layer font defects:** on several pages (notably printed pp. 84–88 and the tree-diagram answers on pp. 101–104) equations and fractions extract as non-Latin glyphs. Rendered images of printed pp. 85 and 87 are clean and legible, so this is an extraction problem in this PDF, not a print defect — but any text-based reuse of those pages will get garbage.
- Series template errors as in the student book: PDF p. 1 says "primary series" / "primary school syllabus" and "equiping"; the title-page text layer carries a stray "PRI" token.
- The guide ends abruptly on printed p. 104 in the middle of the tree-diagram answer key.

### S3 gap note

There is no Secondary 3 (Grade 11) book in this set. Nothing here describes S3 content.

### Method (honest)

Read from the extracted text layer, page by page, using a per-page digest checked against the full text of every unit, plus full-text reads of the worked examples I verified for arithmetic (indices, surds, compound interest, matrices, functions, assumed mean) and of every page where an error is reported. ### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages)

The book has four unnumbered blocks (the contents page names them without unit numbers).

**Numerical concepts and commercial arithmetic (printed pp. 1–18).** Indices — repeated multiplication, base and exponent, laws of indices including fractional, zero and negative indices (pp. 1–4); surds — what a surd is, simplest form, adding/subtracting, simplifying, multiplication/division, rationalising denominators (pp. 5–8); commercial arithmetic — foreign exchange rates (pp. 9–10), simple interest (pp. 10–11), income tax and how it is calculated from a table (p. 12), compound interest including the formula, present value and appreciation/depreciation (pp. 13–16), hire purchase (pp. 17–18). Exercises 1–12.

**Geometric figures and Pythagoras' theorem (printed pp. 19–32).** Review of surface area of shapes (pp. 19–20); coordinate geometry — plotting points, the equation of a line, gradient of increasing/decreasing lines (pp. 20–25); proving Pythagoras' theorem — presented as tasks, "Proof 2" and "Proof 3" are sketched as prompts for learners to complete, not given as full proofs (pp. 26–27); the unit circle — sin, cos and tan read off a circle of radius 1 as the angle grows, including tan θ = sin θ / cos θ (pp. 27–32). Exercise 3 closes the block.

**Algebra (printed pp. 33–68).** Algebraic expressions and equations, expansion by area models, quadratic expressions (pp. 33–37); solving quadratic equations by factorisation (pp. 38–39); vectors — translation review from Book 1, position vectors, vector notation (boldface), addition (pp. 39–42); sets — describing sets, universal and empty sets, subsets, complements, set operations (pp. 43–46); Venn diagrams, including three-set survey problems (pp. 47–53); matrices — what a matrix is, order, equality, addition/subtraction, scalar multiplication, matrix multiplication, identity matrix, determinant of a 2×2 matrix, inverse matrices via AB = I, solving simultaneous equations using matrices (pp. 54–60); functions — definition, notation, composite functions, inverse functions, one-to-one functions and how to find an inverse (pp. 61–68). Exercises 1–13.

**Statistics (printed pp. 69–82).** Mean, mode and median reviewed (pp. 69–70); assumed mean, including its use for variance and standard deviation later (pp. 71–74); cumulative frequency — tables, ogive (cumulative frequency graph), reading medians, quartiles and percentiles from the graph (pp. 76–82). Exercises 1–3. The book ends mid-exercise on printed p. 82 (a cumulative-frequency word problem), with no closing section or back matter.

### What this book does NOT teach (proven from a full read + full-text search)

No complex numbers, no calculus, no logarithms as a topic (the number e is not mentioned in this student book), no probability at all (probability was covered in Secondary 1 and is not revisited here), no bearings, no transformations beyond the translation review inside vectors, no inequalities, no trigonometric equations or trig graphs beyond the unit circle, no permutations or combinations, no binomial theorem, no circle equations, no loci, no differential equations, no matrices beyond 2×2 (no 3×3 determinants or inverses).

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- **Printed p. 9, foreign-exchange text vs table:** the text says "In 2013, 100 SSP = KSh 79, this means that 1 SSP = KSh 0.79", but the table directly below says "100 SSP — Kenya Shilling Ksh 80". The two figures (79 and 80) disagree; one of them is wrong.
- **Printed p. 57:** "When two matrices A and B are such that AB = I then A is said to the inverse of B" — missing the word "be" ("said to [be] the inverse").
- **Printed pp. 26–27:** the Pythagoras proofs are not actually given — "Proof 2" asks "How does this help to prove Pythagoras' theorem?" and "Proof 3" tells learners to "use the figure below to derive Pythagoras' theorem". A learner using only this book never sees a complete proof (the Teacher's Guide supplies Bhaskara's proofs instead).
- Worked examples checked and found correct: compound interest, printed p. 13 (SSP 10,000 at 5% for 3 years → interest SSP 1,576.25); present value, printed p. 14 (SSP 1,000 at 4.5% compounded half-yearly for 7 years → SSP 732.34; daily compounding → SSP 729.80; the simple-interest comparison figure SSP 760.46 is also right); the furniture matrix on printed p. 54 (10 apartments × 4 chairs = 40, etc.); the determinant definition on printed p. 57 (ad − bc). No arithmetic slip was found in the worked examples I verified.

### Held-copy defects

- No missing or blank pages; the text layer is good throughout and all diagrams are present as images.
- Series template errors as in Book 1: PDF p. 1 says "primary series" / "primary school syllabus" and "equiping"; the title-page text layer carries a stray "PRI" token.
- The book ends abruptly mid-exercise on printed p. 82.

### S3 gap note

There is no Secondary 3 (Grade 11) book in this set. Nothing here describes S3 content.

### Companion verdict: YES — it companions the Secondary 2 Student's Book

Same four blocks in the same order, keyed to the same student-book exercises, with syllabus front-matter (Learn About, key inquiry questions, learning outcomes, competencies, links to other subjects) at the head of each unit and teaching notes plus worked answers throughout. Two things to note:

- **It supplies material the student book only sketches:** Bhaskara's first and second proofs of Pythagoras' theorem (printed pp. 19–20) where the student book leaves the proofs as learner tasks; a treatment of the unit circle in the complex plane (printed p. 21); and a separate "Trigonometric ratios" section (printed p. 24).

A teacher gets no answer key for the book's closing exercise.

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages are the GUIDE's own pagination)

**Front matter (printed pp. 1–4).** Introduction (p. 1); "About this guide" (p. 2); "Making Classroom Assessment" (pp. 3–4).

**Unit 1 — Numerical concepts and commercial arithmetic (printed pp. 5–14).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 5–6, including a "Links to other subjects" note). Teaching notes and answers for: indices and fractional/zero/negative indices, laws of indices (pp. 7–8); surds — irrational numbers, operations with radicals, simplifying, multiplication/division, rationalising denominators (pp. 8–10); commercial arithmetic — foreign-exchange task, simple interest, income tax, compound interest with a worked year-by-year investigation (pp. 10–13); appreciation/depreciation and hire purchase (pp. 13–14).

**Unit 2 — Geometric figures and Pythagoras' theorem (printed pp. 15–24).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 15–16); surface area of cones and coordinate geometry (pp. 17–18); proving Pythagoras' theorem — Bhaskara's first proof (area by components) and second proof (pp. 19–20); the unit circle, including the complex-plane form (pp. 21–23); trigonometric ratios and Exercise 3 guidance (p. 24).

**Unit 3 — Algebra (printed pp. 25–44).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 25–26); algebraic expressions, expansion and factorisation (pp. 26–28); solving quadratic equations (p. 28); vectors — translation vectors and position vectors (pp. 29–30); sets and Venn diagrams, with worked survey activities and answers (pp. 31–37); matrices — discussion prompts, scalar multiplication, determinant, solving simultaneous equations with matrices (pp. 38–40); functions — straight-line graphs, composite functions, inverse functions (pp. 41–44).

Ends without an answer key for Exercise 3 (see above).

### What this book does NOT teach

Same absences as its student book (no complex numbers, calculus, logarithms as a topic, probability, bearings, inequalities, permutations), plus nothing beyond the extras listed above.

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- The compound-interest investigation on printed p. 12 (SSP 1,000 at 10%: year 1 interest SSP 100 → SSP 1,100; year 2 interest SSP 110 → SSP 1,210) was checked and is correct. The Bhaskara proofs on printed pp. 19–20 were read and are sound in outline.

- The guide ends without closing matter on printed p. 59, with the final exercise unanswered.

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages)

**Front matter (PDF pp. 1–8, unnumbered).** Series blurb page, "How to take care of your books", title/imprint page, Foreword, Table of Contents (three pages — the longest contents in the set).

**Unit 1 — Limits, rational functions (printed pp. 1–66).** Why limits matter, introduced with a maximum-area rectangle problem (pp. 1–2); definition of a limit (p. 3); estimating limits numerically from tables and graphically (pp. 3–13); limits of non-continuous functions, functions defined by two expressions, limits that exist where the function is not defined (pp. 5–13); unbounded limits and oscillating behaviour (pp. 14–16); determining limits by direct substitution, with limit theorems (pp. 19–23); trigonometric limits (pp. 23–24); indirect substitution / simplifying by common factors (pp. 25–28); rationalising (pp. 29–31); one-sided limits (pp. 32–36); continuity of a function and continuity on an interval (pp. 37–40); trigonometric limits and the squeeze theorem, including lim(x→0) sin x / x = 1 (pp. 43–49); limits at infinity (pp. 50–53); limit and gradient of curves — deriving the derivative from secants (pp. 54–58); limit and area under a curve, including summation and five-rectangle approximations (pp. 59–65). Exercises 1.1–1.11, plus a "Further reading" prompt on Desmos and GeoGebra (p. 66).

**Unit 2 — Trigonometry 2 (printed pp. 67–87).** Trigonometric equations and functions, recalling identities; radian measure (pp. 67–69); simple trigonometric equations, solved graphically and arithmetically (pp. 70–75); solving quadratic trigonometric equations, by formula and by graph (pp. 76–79); using identities in equations with more than one trigonometric function; sum and difference of angle identities, Pythagoras identity (pp. 79–87). Exercises 2.1–2.3.

**Unit 3 — Calculus 3 (printed pp. 88–125).** Product and quotient rules, with the quotient rule derived from the product rule as a task (pp. 88–91); implicit differentiation (pp. 91–92); derivatives of exponential and logarithmic functions, including ln as log base e (pp. 92–93); derivatives of trigonometric functions proved from first principles using the two standard limits (pp. 94–102); integration — area under curves, area by horizontal elements, integration by substitution for powers of linear functions (pp. 103–112); integrals of exponential and logarithmic functions (p. 113); integration of trigonometric functions, with a section on inverse functions and their graphs (pp. 114–122); application of integration to kinematics (pp. 123–125). Exercises 3.1–3.7.

**Unit 4 — Partial fractions (printed pp. 126–148).** What partial fractions are and why they help integration (pp. 126–127); linear factors in the denominator, by comparing coefficients and by substituting values that eliminate a constant (pp. 128–135); quadratic factors in the denominator, including repeated linear and irreducible quadratic factors (pp. 136–142); application of partial fractions in integration (pp. 143–146); expressing partial fractions as a single fraction (pp. 147–148). Exercises 4.1–4.4.

**Unit 5 — Vectors (printed pp. 149–177).** Scalar and vector quantities, i, j and k notation (pp. 149–150); position vectors in 3 dimensions, equal vectors, addition of vectors in the plane and in 3D (pp. 151–157); magnitude of a vector (pp. 158–160); vector equation of a line and its Cartesian form (pp. 162–166); the scalar (dot) product, its properties, perpendicular vectors, scalar product on the Cartesian plane (pp. 167–173); applications — angle between two vectors, components of one vector in the direction of another (pp. 173–177). Exercises 5.1–5.6.

**Unit 6 — Complex numbers (printed pp. 178–195).** Graphical representation on the Argand diagram (pp. 178–181); polar form, x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ (pp. 182–184); De Moivre's theorem, derived from the binomial expansion, used to derive trigonometric identities (pp. 185–189); De Moivre's theorem and roots of complex numbers, including the exponential form z = re^(iθ) (pp. 190–195). Exercises 6.1–6.4. The book ends mid-exercise on printed p. 195 (Exercise 6.4, question 9), with no closing section or back matter.

### What this book does NOT teach (proven from a full read + full-text search)

No matrices or determinants, no differential equations (implicit differentiation is taught, but no equation of the form dy/dx = … is ever solved), no probability or statistics at all, no linear programming, no sets or Venn diagrams, no binomial theorem as a topic (it is mentioned once, as the source from which De Moivre's theorem is derived), no loci, no bearings or transformations, no sequences and series as a topic (summation appears only inside the limit-and-area section), no surds as a topic. Integration by parts is mentioned in passing (p. 103: "Integration by substitution, and integration by part…") but never actually taught — no worked example of integration by parts appears anywhere in the book.

### Relation to the regular Secondary 4 Mathematics book (do not assume a subset)

The Additional Mathematics book is about twice as long (195 vs 96 printed pages) and goes deeper where the two overlap — limits before differentiation, De Moivre for complex numbers, dot product and line equations for vectors, and far more calculus. But it is NOT a superset either: the regular book alone teaches area estimation (mid-ordinate and trapezium rules), circle equations, trigonometric graphs, linear inequalities/linear programming, and permutations/combinations, none of which appear here. Each book has topics the other lacks; the overlap is complex numbers, vectors, trigonometry and calculus at different depths.

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- **Printed p. 31, limit table for sin x / x (read from the rendered image):** the table gives sin(0.001) as 0.00099 and f(x) = sin x / x as 0.9999. To four decimal places sin(0.001) ≈ 0.0010 and the ratio ≈ 1.0000. The printed values are wrongly rounded/truncated (the conclusion, lim(x→0) sin x / x = 1, is still right).
- **Printed p. 130–131, partial fractions worked example:** the working is muddled even though the final answers (A = 3/4, B = −1/4 for 2x/((x + 3)(3x + 1))) are correct. On p. 130 it says "Coefficient of x² on L.H.S equal to coefficient of x² of RHS" although there is no x² term anywhere, and a line reads "A = +3/4B" (a garbled conflation of A = 3/4 and B = −1/4). On p. 131 the equation is misstated as "2x(3x + 1) = A(3x + 1) + B(x + 3)" — the left side should be just 2x — and the substitution lines are garbled ("2(−1/3).0 = A.O + B"). A learner following the printed steps cannot reproduce the answers.
- **Printed p. 189, deriving cos 3θ:** the binomial expansion is introduced as "(x + y)³ = x³ + 3x²y + 3xy² + y²" — the last term should be **y³**, not y². In the same derivation "−3 cos x sin²θ" appears where it should be cos θ, and a line equating real parts contains imaginary terms ("cos 3θ − 3i cos²θ sin θ − i(…) = …"), so the printed working does not actually prove what it claims. The final result, cos 3θ = 4cos³θ − 3cosθ, is correct.
- **Printed p. 189, Exercise 6.3, question 3:** "Find the value of sinsin 3θ in terms of powers of sin θ" — "sinsin" is a typo (duplicated word).
- **Printed p. 194, cube roots of 8 (confirmed from the rendered image — these are printed errors, not extraction artefacts):** the page writes "z³ = √8 e^…" and "z^(1/3) = √8 e^…" — the cube root of 8 is 2, not √8 (√8 is the square root); a stray line reads "z₁ = 8" where it should be part of z³ = 8; for k = 1 the root is printed as "2e^(12π/3)" — the exponent should be 2π/3 (12π/3 = 4π, which would give 2, not −1 + i√3); for k = 2 it is printed as "2e^(14π/2)" — the exponent should be 4π/3 — and the value is given as −1 + i√3, but the third cube root of 8 is **−1 − i√3**. So the k = 2 root repeats the k = 1 value with the wrong sign on the imaginary part. The first root (k = 0, z₀ = 2) is correct.
- **Printed pp. 174–175, Example 14 (angle between two vectors):** the book computes "a·b = (2, 3, 4)·(3, 0, 4) = 2×3 + 0×3 + 4×4 = 16" — the sum is wrong: 6 + 0 + 16 = **22**, not 16. The very next line then says "a·b = 22", and on p. 175 "Substituting a.b = 16 … we get 22 = 5√29 cos θ" — the sentence contradicts itself. The final answer θ = 35.2° is correct, because it uses 22 (cos θ = 22/(5√29) ≈ 0.8171). The Teacher's Guide gives the same example correctly as 16 + 6 = 22.
- **Printed p. 115:** "The knowledge on inverse of functions ids used to determine the integral of trigonometric functions" — "ids" is a typo for "is", and the inverse-function graphs that follow sit oddly inside the integration section (they do not lead to any integral).

### Held-copy defects

- **PDF p. 39 / printed p. 31** (the sin x / x table and graph) is an image-heavy page with almost no text layer; read visually.
- No missing pages; the text layer is otherwise good.
- Series template errors as in the other books: PDF p. 1 says "primary series" / "primary school syllabus" and "equiping"; the title-page text layer carries a stray "PRI" token.
- The book ends abruptly mid-exercise on printed p. 195.

### S3 gap note

There is no Secondary 3 (Grade 11) book in this set, and this book repeatedly refers back to earlier work — "knowledge of trigonometry learnt in Additional [Mathematics]" (p. 67), "in the previous units in calculus we have discussed…" (p. 88), "the concept of complex numbers was introduced in additional [mathematics]" (p. 178) — i.e. it assumes S3 Additional Mathematics units (trigonometry 1, calculus 1–2, complex numbers 1) that are not in hand. Do not reconstruct that S3 material from this book; the gap is real and must be filled from the missing S3 books, not inferred.

### Companion verdict: YES — it companions the Additional Mathematics Student's Book (NOT the regular Mathematics book)

Same six units in the same order — Limits, Trigonometry 2, Calculus 3, Partial Fractions, Vectors, Complex Numbers — keyed to the same student-book exercises (1.1–1.11, 2.1–2.3, 3.1–3.7, 4.1–4.4, 5.1–5.6, 6.1–6.4). It never strays into the regular Mathematics book's topics (no area estimation, circle equations, linear programming or permutations). Its structure differs from the other three guides: each unit is organised as "Learn about / Key inquiry questions / Learning-Teaching Materials / Learning-Teaching Activities / ANSWERS" rather than the syllabus "Learn About" blocks plus interleaved expected answers, and it is much more condensed (93 printed pages against the student book's 195). Two things to note:

- **It adds a section the student book does not have:** "Limits and series" (printed pp. 24–26), working with S = Σ results. There is no matching section in the student book (which covers summation only inside limit-and-area, printed pp. 60–61).
- **Its Exercise 6.4 answers do not line up with the student book's Exercise 6.4** (see slips below): six answers are present but misnumbered, and four of the nine questions (1, 2, 3, 5) have no answer at all.

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages are the GUIDE's own pagination)

**Front matter (printed pp. 1–3).** Introduction (p. 1); "About this guide" (p. 2); "Making Classroom Assessment" (p. 3).

**Unit 1 — Limits (printed pp. 4–26).** Syllabus front-matter and materials (pp. 4–5); teaching activities and answers for estimating limits numerically and graphically (pp. 5–12); direct and indirect substitution, continuity (pp. 13–17); trigonometric limits, squeeze theorem, calculating trigonometric limits (pp. 19–22); limits at infinity and Exercise 1.7 answers (p. 23); limits and gradient, limits and series, limits and area under a curve (pp. 23–26).

**Unit 2 — Trigonometry 2 (printed pp. 27–36).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 27–29); solution of simple trigonometric equations, with answers (pp. 30–33); solving quadratic trigonometric equations, graphical and arithmetic methods (pp. 34–35); using identities, with answers for Exercise 2.3 (pp. 35–36).

**Unit 3 — Calculus 3 (printed pp. 37–55).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 37–39); the product rule derived from first principles via a rectangle-area argument, and the quotient rule (pp. 41–44); differentiation by product and quotient rules, implicit differentiation, derivatives of exponential/logarithmic and trigonometric functions (pp. 44–48); integration — area under curves, integration by substitution, exponential/logarithmic and trigonometric integrals (pp. 48–54); application of integration to kinematics, deriving displacement/velocity/acceleration for constant acceleration (p. 55).

**Unit 4 — Partial fractions (printed pp. 56–62).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 56–57); teaching activities for linear and quadratic denominators, application to integration, recombining into a single fraction (pp. 58–59); answers for Exercises 4.1–4.4, including a note on improper fractions (numerator of greater power than denominator) (pp. 60–62).

**Unit 5 — Vectors (printed pp. 63–83).** Syllabus front-matter and materials, including a ticker timer and a "3-dimensional method box" (pp. 63–65); teaching activities for position vectors, addition, magnitude, scalar multiplication, vector equation of a line, Cartesian form, scalar product (pp. 65–68); worked answers and tasks for Exercises 5.1–5.6, including the angle-between-vectors example (pp. 68–83).

**Unit 6 — Complex numbers (printed pp. 84–93).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 84–86); graphical representation, polar form, De Moivre's theorem, solving quadratics with complex solutions (pp. 86–88); answers for Exercises 6.1–6.3 and the roots section (pp. 88–93). The guide ends on printed p. 93 mid-answer-list (the fifth-roots answers and "6. 2 + 11i"), with no closing section.

### What this book does NOT teach

Same absences as its student book (no matrices, differential equations, probability/statistics, linear programming, sets/Venn), plus nothing beyond the extras listed above.

It also does not answer the student book's Exercise 6.3 question 1, which asks to show sin 3θ = 3 cos²θ sin θ − sin³θ. On the same page, "z⁴ = (cos θ + i sin θ)⁴ = cos 4x + i sin 4θ" — "cos 4x" should be cos 4θ.

The guide prints six numbered answers: "1. 4096" — the answer to student **Q4** (z¹² = 2¹² = 4096), not Q1; "2." — a garbled five-root set (arguments 5π/8, 9π/8, 13π/8 …) that matches no student question; "3. −64√3 − 64i" — the answer to student **Q6**; "4. −16" — the answer to student **Q7**; "5." — the five 5th roots with arguments 6°, 78°, 150°, 222°, 294° — the answer to student **Q8**, with the 2^(1/5) modulus shown on all five roots but z₂ listed before z₁; "6. 2 + 11i" — the answer to student **Q9**. Questions 1, 2, 3 and 5 have no answers in this guide. [CHECK 2026-11-05: the filing's earlier version of this slip said only two answers were printed and that Q6, Q7 and Q9 were unanswered — wrong; the guide prints six answers and Q6, Q7, Q8 and Q9 are answered, under shifted numbers.]
- **Printed p. 82 (guide), angle-between-vectors example — guide is RIGHT where the student book is wrong:** the guide writes "a·b = (2, 3, 4)·(3, 0, 4) = 2 × 3 + 0 × 3 + 4 × 4 = 16 + 6 = 22" and gets θ = 35.2°. The student book (printed p. 174) writes the same sum as "= 16". A teacher using the guide will teach the correct value; a learner using only the student book will meet the wrong intermediate step. (The guide's "16 + 6" grouping silently drops the 0 × 3 term, but the total 22 is correct.)

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages)

**Front matter (PDF pp. 1–6, unnumbered).** Series blurb page, "How to take care of your books", title/imprint page, Foreword, Table of Contents.

**Unit 1 — Complex numbers (printed pp. 1–19).** Why √−16 has no real answer; complex numbers as a + bi with i = √−1 (pp. 1–2); the Argand diagram — real axis, imaginary axis, plotting points (pp. 3–6); conjugates (pp. 7–8); operations — adding, subtracting, multiplying, dividing by conjugates (pp. 8–11); polar form — modulus, argument, r(cos θ + i sin θ) (pp. 12–16); uses of complex numbers — solving quadratics, electronics and electromagnetism, voltage and current as one complex number (pp. 17–18); application questions (pp. 18–19). Exercises 1–8.

**Unit 2 — Measurement and trigonometry (printed pp. 20–45).** Area of basic and irregular shapes (p. 20); approximating an irregular area on grid paper, by counting squares (pp. 21–24); area estimation by the mid-ordinate rule (pp. 25–26) and the trapezium rule (pp. 26–29); equation of a circle — centre at the origin, general centre (a, b), completing the square to find centre and radius, circles through two points and touching the x-axis (pp. 30–40); graphs of trigonometric functions — plotting sin, cos and tan graphs and reading values off them (pp. 41–45). Exercises 1–6.

**Unit 3 — Inequalities and vectors (printed pp. 46–71).** Linear inequalities and their signs (p. 46); solution of linear programming by graphs, with a worked farmer problem (maize vs beans: 20 hectares, SSP 90,000 budget, profit 2000x + 3000y, solved with a search line; maximum profit SSP 41,000 at (19, 1)) (pp. 47–55); permutation and combination formulae, with worked examples and then 30 multiple-choice questions (pp. 56–59); vectors — scalar vs vector quantities, column vectors, position vectors, mid-point, magnitude, multiplication by a scalar, parallel vectors (pp. 58–65); coordinates and vectors in three dimensions — position vectors, magnitude, mid-point in 3D (pp. 66–71). Exercises 1–6.

**Unit 4 — Calculus (printed pp. 72–96).** The derivative as the instantaneous rate of change / slope of a tangent, built from limits of secant gradients (pp. 72–73); derivative of a polynomial from first principles, with the sum, constant-multiple and power rules (pp. 73–77); equation of a tangent and normal to a curve at a point (pp. 78–80); stationary points — maximum and minimum values, distinguishing them by how the gradient changes sign (pp. 81–87); application to kinematics — velocity and acceleration from displacement–time and velocity–time graphs (pp. 88–91); integration — as the reverse of differentiation, general equations of curves from a gradient function (pp. 92–93); integration and area under a curve (pp. 94–96). Exercises 1–5. The book ends mid-exercise on printed p. 96 (Exercise 5, question 5), with no closing section or back matter.

### What this book does NOT teach (proven from a full read + full-text search)

No matrices or determinants, no De Moivre's theorem, no partial fractions, no probability or statistics at all, no logarithms as a topic (the word "logarithm" occurs exactly once, inside a permutation multiple-choice question about arranging the letters of "LOGARITHMS"), no differential equations, no trigonometric equations, no binomial theorem, no radian measure (angles are in degrees throughout), no loci, no bearings, no transformations, no sets/Venn diagrams, no functions as a topic (composite/inverse functions are absent). Integration is taught only as the reverse of differentiation and as area under a curve — no integration by substitution or by parts, and no integrals of exponential, logarithmic or trigonometric functions (those are in the Additional Mathematics book).

### Is the shorter G12 Maths book a subset of the Additional Mathematics book? NO — proven from the contents

The regular Mathematics book is 96 printed pages against Additional Mathematics' 195, but it is not an extract of it. Only the regular book teaches: area estimation (mid-ordinate and trapezium rules), circle equations, trigonometric graphs, linear inequalities and linear programming, and permutations/combinations. Only the Additional Mathematics book teaches: limits, trigonometric equations, product/quotient/implicit differentiation, derivatives and integrals of exponential/logarithmic/trigonometric functions, partial fractions, and De Moivre's theorem. The overlap is complex numbers (basic here, De Moivre there), vectors (2D/3D basics here, dot product and line equations there) and calculus (polynomial differentiation and area here, advanced differentiation and integration there).

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- **Printed p. 17, Exercise 6, question 3(a):** "z = 5(cos 45° − 5i sin 45°" — malformed: a stray "5" before the i and a missing closing bracket. It should read z = 5(cos 45° − i sin 45°).
- **Printed p. 78, tangent example:** "To find the gradient of this curve at a point, say (2, 3), = 6 × (2) + 2 = 14" for the curve y = 3x² + 2x + 1. The gradient 14 at x = 2 is right, but the point (2, 3) is NOT on the curve (at x = 2, y = 17). The point should be (2, 17).
- **Printed p. 85, stationary-points Example 2:** the table for y = −x² + 4x + 6 correctly shows the gradient y′ = 0 at x = 2, but the observation underneath says "Gradient at x = 2 is positive." That contradicts its own table — it should read "Gradient at x = 2 is zero."
- **Printed p. 95, Example 2 — wrong integrand and wrong arithmetic:** "Calculate the area enclosed by the curve y = x² + x, the straight lines x = 1 and x = 3. A = ∫(x³ − 3x² + 4x)dx" — the integrand is not the stated curve (it is the integrand of the previous example). Even for its own integral the arithmetic is wrong: (81/4 − 27 + 9) − (1/4 − 1 + 1) = 2.25 − 0.25 = 2.00, not 2.25 as printed. (The correct answer for the stated curve would be ∫₁³(x² + x)dx = [x³/3 + x²/2]₁³ = (9 + 9/2) − (1/3 + 1/2) = 38/3 ≈ 12.67.)
- **Printed pp. 95–96, Example 3 — three compounding errors:** "Calculate the area bounded by the curve y = x² + x, the straight lines x = 2 and x = 4. A = ∫x² + x dx = [x³/3 − x²/2]" — (i) the antiderivative has the wrong sign: ∫x dx = +x²/2, not −x²/2; (ii) in the evaluation "64/3" is copied as "64/8"; (iii) the final answer is given as "−2/3 sqr units" — an area cannot be negative. The correct value is ∫₂⁴(x² + x)dx = 74/3 ≈ 24.67 square units.
- Checked and found correct: the area example on printed p. 94 (∫₁⁵(x² + 2x)dx = 196/3 = 65 1/3); the linear-programming farmer example (printed pp. 51–54: integer optimum (19, 1), maximum profit SSP 41,000 — re-checked against the constraints x + y ≤ 20, x > y, 4x + 10y ≤ 90); the vector magnitude example on printed p. 68 (|AB| = √[(−2−3)² + (1−6)² + (6−2)²] = √66).

### Held-copy defects

- No missing or blank pages; the text layer is good throughout.
- Series template errors as in the earlier books: PDF p. 1 says "primary series" / "primary school syllabus" and "equiping"; the title-page text layer carries a stray "PRI" token.
- The book ends abruptly mid-exercise on printed p. 96, with no answers, summary or back matter (there is no answer key in the student book at all).

### S3 gap note

There is no Secondary 3 (Grade 11) book in this set. The calculus in this book (derivative from first principles, pp. 72–77) is written as if following earlier calculus units ("In the previous units in calculus we have discussed…" is the Additional Mathematics book's phrasing; this book introduces differentiation from scratch), and nothing here describes S3 content. Do not infer S3 material from this book.

## Secondary 4, Mathematics teacher guide — Teacher's Guide 4 (© 2018; 60 PDF pp.; printed = PDF − 6, folios irregular)

Citation check 2026-11-05: 19 anchors — 18 passed, 1 corrected, 0 not found.

### Book identity

- **File read:** `g12-maths-teacher.pdf` — 60 PDF pages, 4,840,207 bytes.
- **Title as printed:** Secondary Mathematics, Teacher's Guide 4 (Secondary 4).
- **Publisher / imprint as printed:** Mountain Top Publishers; "Secondary Mathematics has been written and developed by Ministry of General Education and Instruction, Government of South Sudan in conjunction with Subjects experts." Property of the Ministry of General Education and Instruction; "THIS BOOK IS NOT FOR SALE". Funded by the Global Partnership for Education. Foreword signed Deng Deng Hoc Yai (Hon.), Minister; approved for use from 4th February, 2019.
- **Edition / year:** ©2018, The Republic of South Sudan, Ministry of General Education and Instruction (title page).
- **ISBN:** NONE printed anywhere in the book. **No individual authors are named** — authorship is credited only to the Ministry collectively.
- **Pages actually read:** all 60 PDF pages (printed pp. 1–52). Printed page = PDF page − 6 for most of the book, but the folio numbering is wrong on two pages (see defects): PDF p. 21 carries folio "2" and PDF p. 22 repeats folio "14", so the printed sequence runs …, 13, 14, "2", 14, 15, …; the final pages sit at offset +8 (PDF p. 60 = printed p. 52).
- **Language:** English throughout.

### Method (honest)

Read from the extracted text layer, page by page, using a per-page digest checked against the full text of every unit, plus full-text reads of the worked examples and answer pages where errors are reported. Two pages (PDF pp. 21–22, the complex-number solutions) were also checked from rendered images, which confirmed the errors and showed the printed folio "2". All complex-number answers reported below were re-computed by hand. Exercise question lists were counted and skimmed, not read word-by-word; all exposition, teaching notes and answer keys were read in full. Keyword searches over the whole text layer back the "does not teach" list.

### Companion verdict: YES — it companions the Secondary 4 Mathematics Student's Book (NOT the Additional Mathematics book)

Same four units in the same order — complex numbers, measurement and trigonometry, inequalities and vectors, calculus — keyed to the same student-book exercises (Exercises 1–8, 1–6, 1–6, 1–5). It does not cover any Additional Mathematics material (no limits, partial fractions or De Moivre). Coverage gaps inside its own book:

- **The 30 permutation multiple-choice questions are never answered.** The student book's Exercise 3 (printed pp. 57–59) is reproduced in full in the guide (printed pp. 37–41) with all four options per question, but no answer key is given anywhere — the guide moves straight on to vectors (Exercise 4). The questions themselves are plainly lifted from an exam-prep source (e.g. "In how many ways can 5 man draw water from 5 taps…", "25 buses are running between two places P and Q…").
- **The final exercise's answers are cut off.** The guide ends on printed p. 52 in the middle of the solutions to the student book's Exercise 5 (integration and area under a curve): question 4 is answered only in part and question 5 is not answered at all. The book simply stops.
- **Folio and numbering defects** (see below) make the printed page numbers unreliable on two pages.

### Contents, unit by unit (own-words summary; printed pages are the GUIDE's own pagination)

**Front matter (printed pp. 1–4).** Introduction (p. 1); "About this guide" (p. 2); "Making Classroom Assessment" (pp. 3–4).

**Complex numbers (printed pp. 5–17).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 5–6); teaching notes on plotting complex numbers on the Cartesian plane (p. 7); a web-style "History of complex numbers" essay, pp. 8–11 (see slip below); teaching notes and solutions for Exercises 1–2 (pp. 11–12); operations on complex numbers, conjugates, multiplying and dividing, with solutions for Exercises 3–5 (pp. 13–14, with the folio errors on the solutions pages); polar form with solutions for Exercises 6–7 (pp. 14–15); application questions, Exercise 8, and a "Real Life Context" note on two-part quantities (pp. 16–17).

**Measurement and trigonometry (printed pp. 18–27).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 18–19); grid-paper area approximation activities and Exercise 1 (pp. 20–21); mid-ordinate and trapezium rules with Exercises 2–3 (pp. 22–23); equation of a circle, including a task that already asks learners to "use integration to find the exact area" — ahead of the calculus unit (p. 24); circle-equation examples and Exercises 4–5 (pp. 24–26); graphs of trigonometric functions and Exercise 6 (pp. 26–27).

**Inequalities and vectors (printed pp. 28–45).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 28–29); linear inequalities with a worked example and Exercise 1 (pp. 30–32); linear programming — a five-areas-of-application essay (railroads, VLSI chip design, WWII planning) and a worked optimisation example, Exercise 2 (pp. 33–36); permutations and combinations — teaching procedure, the fundamental counting principle, and the 30 unanswered multiple-choice questions (pp. 36–41); vectors — Exercises 4–6 with solutions, including 3D vectors (pp. 42–45).

**Calculus (printed pp. 46–52).** Syllabus front-matter (pp. 46–47); derivative of a polynomial with Exercise 1 and a formal definition of the derivative as the slope of a tangent (pp. 48–50); tangent and normal lines with Exercises 2–3 (pp. 50–51); stationary points (p. 51); kinematics and integration/area under a curve with Exercises 4–5, the last answers cut off mid-list (p. 52).

### What this book does NOT teach

Same absences as its student book (no matrices, De Moivre, partial fractions, probability/statistics, logarithms, differential equations), plus nothing beyond the extras listed above.

### Slips and errors found (do not teach as fact)

- **Printed p. 8, "History of complex numbers":** "i is as amazing number. It is the only imaginary number." — **False.** i is the imaginary unit; every number bi with b ≠ 0 is an imaginary number. (The same essay says "every polynomial equation has a solution if complex numbers are used" — the correct statement is that every non-constant polynomial has a root.)
- **Printed p. 15 in the guide's pagination — the solutions page whose folio reads "2" (PDF p. 21), Exercise 4:** question 1 is missing entirely (the list starts with a blank "1." and the first expression is question 2), and two of the printed answers are wrong. The questions are numbered 2–11 and the answers 1–10, aligned in order:
  - Q3 (i)(2i)(−7i) = 14i, but the printed answer is **12i — wrong**.
  - Q4 (3i)(−2 + 6i) = −18 − 6i, but the printed answer is **−8 − 6i — wrong** (real part −18, not −8).
  - Q8 (−3 + 5i)(−5 + 7i) = −20 − 46i, but the printed answer reads **"−2—46i"** — the 0 appears dropped; treat as a misprint.
  - The other answers on that page check out: (−4i)³ = 64i; (−2i)³ = 8i; (6i)(−4i) = 24; (−8i)² = −64; (5−3i)(−8+5i) = −25+49i; (5+5i)(−3−7i) = 20−50i; (−7−4i)(−6−6i) = 18+66i.
- **Printed p. 14 (PDF p. 22), Exercise 5 solutions:** three wrong answers (all re-computed):
  - (a) (4 + 3i)(2 − i) = **11 + 2i**, printed as **14 + 2i — wrong**.
  - (b) (1 + 2i)/(3 − 4i) = **−1/5 + (2/5)i**, printed as **(1/2)i − 5 — wrong**.
  - (d) (1 − 5i)(−9 + 2i) = **1 + 47i**, printed as **1 − 41i — wrong**.
  - Correct on the same page: (c) 7i(−5 + 2i) = −14 − 35i; (e) (1 − 8i)(1 + 8i) = 65.
  - Also on this page: question 1(f) is printed as "(5 + i)2(+6i)" — a bracket is missing; it means (5 + i)(2 + 6i), for which the printed answer 4 + 32i is correct. And the whole of Exercise 5 is headed "Dividing complex numbers" although only one of its six items (b) is a division; the rest are multiplications.
  - Exercise 5, question 2(c): the printed "answer" is just the question restated, 5/(−10i), instead of the simplified value i/2.
- **Printed p. 14 (PDF p. 22), Exercise 6, question 4 answer:** "p(3, 6.3i)" — a point's coordinates cannot contain i; it should be the point (3, 6.3), i.e. the complex number 3 + 6.3i.

## Band C cross-notes (for course building)

- **Ladder.** S1 natural numbers/factors/proportion, fractions/decimals/percentages, standard form, profit/loss, measurement, geometry/trigonometry (trig ratios, elevation/depression), algebra (expand, factorise, equations, simultaneous equations, inequalities), statistics and probability (mean/mode/median, tree diagrams) → S2 indices, surds, commercial arithmetic, coordinate geometry, Pythagoras (proofs as learner tasks — the guide supplies Bhaskara's proofs), unit circle, quadratics by factorisation, vectors, sets/Venn, 2×2 matrices, functions (composite/inverse), assumed mean, cumulative frequency/ogive → S4 regular: complex numbers (basic), area estimation, circle equations, trig graphs, linear programming, permutations/combinations, vectors (2D/3D), calculus (differentiation from first principles, area by integration) → S4 Additional: limits, trig equations, calculus 3, partial fractions, vectors (dot product, line equations), complex numbers (polar form, De Moivre, roots).
- **The S3 hole shapes S4.** The S4 Additional book assumes S3 units (Trigonometry 1, Calculus 1–2, Complex Numbers 1) that are not in hand; do not reconstruct that S3 material from the S4 books. The S4 regular book introduces differentiation from scratch, so it stands alone better.
- **Regular vs Additional is NOT a subset.** Only the regular book teaches area estimation (mid-ordinate/trapezium rules), circle equations, trig graphs, linear programming and permutations/combinations; only the Additional book teaches limits, trig equations, advanced differentiation/integration, partial fractions and De Moivre. Merge both books' stock into the lessons, tagged by book.
- **Guide gaps to carry.** S1 guide never covers inequalities (promised, not delivered); S2 guide gives no answers for the closing cumulative-frequency exercise; S4 Maths guide reproduces the 30 permutation MCQs with no key; S4 Additional guide's Exercise 6.4 answers are misnumbered (six answers printed, for student questions 4, 6, 7, 8, 9 and one garbled set; questions 1, 2, 3, 5 unanswered).
- **What to carry, corrected.** S1 student p. 108 "y = 3" for y + 5 < 8 (correct y < 3); S2 guide p. 6 e = 2.78 (correct ≈ 2.718) and p. 11 £1 = SSP 17.50 (correct 175); S4 Maths student pp. 95–96 area examples (correct values 38/3 ≈ 12.67 and 74/3 ≈ 24.67); S4 Additional student p. 174 dot product 22 (not 16) and p. 194 cube roots of 8 (2, −1 + i√3, −1 − i√3); S4 Maths guide Exercise 4/5 complex-number answers (14i, −18 − 6i, 11 + 2i, −1/5 + (2/5)i, 1 + 47i).


## Gaps

- **No Secondary 3 (Grade 11) books at all.** 4 link-only (the whole S3 set: student + teacher, regular + Additional — the record's 4 link-only books). The S4 Additional book assumes S3 units (Trigonometry 1, Calculus 1–2, Complex Numbers 1) not in hand. Nothing in this filing describes S3 content, and no S3 material should be inferred from the S4 books.
- **No complex numbers before S4** (basic in the S4 regular book, polar form and De Moivre in the S4 Additional book). **No differential equations anywhere** (implicit differentiation is taught in S4 Additional, but no equation of the form dy/dx = … is ever solved). **No probability distributions anywhere** (S1 teaches single-event probability; S2 and S4 teach none). No matrices beyond 2×2 (S2 only). No logarithms as a topic (the S2 guide states e = 2.78 — wrong; correct ≈ 2.718). No calculus before S4. No functions as a topic before S2. No sets/Venn before S2 (S1 teaches none). No bearings beyond S1. No transformations beyond S1 (S2 reviews translation only inside vectors). No sequences/series as a topic (summation appears only inside the S4 Additional limit-and-area section). Integration by parts is mentioned once (S4 Additional p. 103) but never taught.
- **Primary ceilings.** No negative-number arithmetic as a topic in primary (negatives appear only inside algebra answers from P6); no coordinates before P6 (plotting only, no exercises set); no 3-D shapes in P1 despite the Unit 3 content map promising them; no probability before P7; no decimals as a topic before P4 (P3 shows decimal litres only as conversion results); no multiplication or division at all in P3; no area/perimeter formulas before P4 (P2 teaches length only).
- **No answers sections.** No student book in the set carries an answers section; the P1 guide carries no answer key at all; the S4 Maths guide leaves the 30 permutation MCQs unanswered; the S2 guide leaves the closing cumulative-frequency exercise unanswered.
